13	      Composite ML-KEM for use in X.509 Public Key Infrastructure
14	                  draft-ietf-lamps-pq-composite-kem-20

16	Abstract

18	   This document defines combinations of US NIST ML-KEM in hybrid with
19	   traditional algorithms RSA-OAEP, ECDH, X25519, and X448.  These
20	   combinations are tailored to meet security best practices and
21	   regulatory guidelines.  Composite ML-KEM is applicable in any
22	   application that uses X.509 or PKIX data structures that accept ML-
23	   KEM, but where the operator wants extra protection against breaks or
24	   catastrophic bugs in ML-KEM.

142	1.  Introduction

144	   The advent of quantum computing poses a significant threat to current
145	   cryptographic systems because traditional cryptographic key
146	   establishment algorithms in common use -- namely the
147	   Rivest–Shamir–Adleman cryptosystem in the Optimal Asymmetric
148	   Encryption Padding mode (RSA-OAEP), and elliptic curve Diffie-Hellman
149	   (ECDH) -- will become vulnerable to quantum attacks.  Unlike previous
150	   migrations between cryptographic algorithms, this migration gives us
151	   the foresight that traditional cryptographic algorithms will be
152	   broken in the future, but will remain strong in the interim, the only
153	   uncertainty is around the timing.  But there are also some novel
154	   challenges.  For instance, the aggressive migration timelines may
155	   require deploying Post-Quantum Cryptographic algorithms (PQC) before
156	   their implementations have been fully hardened or certified, and
157	   dual-algorithm data protection may be desirable over a longer time
158	   period to hedge against security vulnerabilities and other
159	   implementation flaws in the new implementations.

161	   Cautious implementers may opt to combine cryptographic algorithms in
162	   such a way that an attacker would need to break all of them
163	   simultaneously to compromise the protected data.  These mechanisms
164	   are referred to as "Post-Quantum / Traditional (PQ/T) Hybrids"
165	   [RFC9794].  Further discussion of PQ/T Hybrids can be found in
166	   Section 9.1.

168	   This specification defines a specific instantiation of the PQ/T
169	   Hybrid paradigm called "composite" where multiple cryptographic
170	   algorithms are combined to form a single key encapsulation mechanism
171	   (KEM).  The composite KEM presents a single public key and ciphertext
172	   such that it can be treated as a single atomic algorithm at the
173	   protocol level.  This provides a property referred to as "protocol
174	   backwards compatibility" since it can be applied to protocols that
175	   are not explicitly hybrid-aware.  The idea of a composite was first
176	   presented in [Bindel2017].  Composite algorithms retain some security
177	   even if one of their component algorithms is broken, which is
178	   discussed in detail in Section 9.  This specification creates PQ/T
179	   Hybrids with the Module-Lattice-based Key Encapsulation Mechanism
180	   (ML-KEM), defined in [FIPS.203] as the PQ component.  Instantiations
181	   of the composite ML-KEM scheme are provided based on ML-KEM, RSA-OAEP
182	   and ECDH.  The full list of algorithms registered by this
183	   specification is in Section 6.  Application backwards compatibility
184	   in the sense of upgraded systems continuing to interoperate with
185	   legacy systems is not provided by the mechanisms defined in this
186	   specification; this is discussed further in Section 10.2.

188	   Certain jurisdictions have recommended that ML-KEM be used
189	   exclusively within a PQ/T hybrid framework.  The use of a composite
190	   scheme provides a straightforward implementation of hybrid solutions
191	   compatible with (and advocated by) some governments and cybersecurity
192	   agencies [BSI2021], [ANSSI2024].

194	   In some situations it might be possible to add Post-Quantum, via a
195	   PQ/T Hybrid, to an already audited and compliant solution without
196	   invalidating the existing certification, whereas a full replacement
197	   of the traditional cryptography would almost certainly incur
198	   regulatory and compliance delays.  In other words, PQ/T Hybrids can
199	   allow for deploying Post-Quantum Cryptography before the PQ modules
200	   and operational procedures are fully audited and certified.  This,
201	   more than any other requirement, is what motivates the large number
202	   of algorithm combinations in this specification: The intention is to
203	   provide a stepping stone from which any cryptographic algorithm an
204	   organization has deployed today can evolve or transition.

206	   While this specification registers a large number of composite
207	   algorithms, it is expected that organizations will choose to deploy a
208	   single composite algorithm, or a small number of composite
209	   algorithms, that meets the needs of their environment, and very few
210	   implementers will need concern themselves with the entire list.  This
211	   specification does not specify any mandatory-to-implement algorithms,
212	   but Section 10.3 provides a short-list of recommended composite
213	   algorithms for common use-cases.

215	   Composite ML-KEM is applicable in any PKIX-related application that
216	   would otherwise use ML-KEM.

218	1.1.  Conventions and Terminology

220	   The key words "MUST", "MUST NOT", "REQUIRED", "SHALL", "SHALL NOT",
221	   "SHOULD", "SHOULD NOT", "RECOMMENDED", "NOT RECOMMENDED", "MAY", and
222	   "OPTIONAL" in this document are to be interpreted as described in
223	   BCP 14 [RFC2119] [RFC8174] when, and only when, they appear in all
224	   capitals, as shown here.  These words may also appear in this
225	   document in lower case as plain English words, absent their normative
226	   meanings.

228	   This specification is consistent with all terminology from [RFC9794].
229	   Some relevant definitions from [RFC9794] are copied here for easier
230	   reading.  In addition, the following terms are used in this
231	   specification:

233	   *ALGORITHM*: The usage of the term "algorithm" within this
234	   specification generally refers to any function which has a registered
235	   Object Identifier (OID) for use within an ASN.1 AlgorithmIdentifier.

237	   *APPLICATION BACKWARDS COMPATIBILITY*: A property indicating whether
238	   an upgraded and non-upgraded application can successfully establish
239	   communication.

241	   *COMBINER*: A combiner specifies how multiple shared secret keys are
242	   combined into a single shared secret key.

244	   *COMPOSITE CRYPTOGRAPHIC ELEMENT*: [RFC9794] defines composites as: A
245	   cryptographic element that incorporates multiple component
246	   cryptographic elements of the same type for use in a multi-algorithm
247	   scheme, such that the resulting composite cryptographic element is
248	   exposed as a singular interface of the same type as the component
249	   cryptographic elements.  For example this could be an asymmetric
250	   algorithm such as "ML-KEM-768" or "RSA-OAEP".

252	   *DER:* Distinguished Encoding Rules as defined in [X.690].

254	   *ECDH*: the Elliptic Curve Diffie-Hellman key agreement scheme
255	   defined in section 5.7.1.2 of [SP.800-56Ar3].

257	   *KEM:* A key encapsulation mechanism as defined in Section 2.

259	   *PKI:* Public Key Infrastructure, as defined in [RFC5280].

261	   *POST-QUANTUM TRADITIONAL (PQ/T) HYBRID SCHEME*: [RFC9794] defines a
262	   PQ/T Hybrid Scheme as: A multi-algorithm scheme where at least one
263	   component algorithm is a post-quantum algorithm and at least one is a
264	   traditional algorithm.

266	   *PROTOCOL BACKWARDS COMPATIBILITY*: A property whereby a new feature
267	   can be added to a protocol without requiring any changes to the
268	   protocol's specification and only minimal changes to its
269	   implementations (such as adding new identifiers).  Typically this
270	   means that the new feature fits within a defined extension point of
271	   the protocol instead of requiring a structural change to the
272	   protocol.  This is notable because many PQ/T Hybrids require
273	   modification of the protocol to make it "hybrid aware", whereas this
274	   specification presents as a standalone algorithm and thus can take
275	   advantage of existing cryptographic agility mechanisms.

277	   *ML-KEM*: The Module-Lattice-based Key Encapsulation Mechanism
278	   algorithm defined in [FIPS.203]

280	   *RSA*: The Rivest-Shamir-Adleman cryptosystem, used in this
281	   specification as the RSA-OAEP (Optimal Asymmetric Encryption Padding)
282	   scheme defined in [RFC8017].

284	   *SHARED SECRET KEY:* A value established between two communicating
285	   parties for use as cryptographic key material suitable for direct use
286	   by symmetric cryptographic algorithms.  This specification is
287	   concerned with shared secrets established via public key
288	   cryptographic operations.

290	   *X25519 and X448:* The Edwards Curve Diffie-Hellman scheme defined in
291	   [RFC7748] with paramater sets X25519 and X448.

293	1.2.  Notation

295	   The algorithm descriptions use python-like syntax.  The following
296	   symbols deserve special mention:

298	   *  || represents concatenation of two byte arrays.

300	   *  [:] represents byte array slicing.

302	   *  (a, b) represents a pair of values a and b.  Typically this
303	      indicates that a function returns multiple values; the exact
304	      conveyance mechanism -- tuple, struct, output parameters, etc --
305	      is left to the implementer.

307	   *  (a, _): represents a pair of values where one -- the second one in
308	      this case -- is ignored.

310	   *  func(a) -> b: represents a function named func that takes a as
311	      input and produces b.

313	   *  Func<TYPE>(): represents a function that is parametrized by <TYPE>
314	      meaning that the function's implementation will have minor
315	      differences depending on the underlying TYPE.  Typically this
316	      means that a function will need to look up different constants or
317	      use different underlying cryptographic primitives depending on
318	      which composite algorithm it is implementing.

320	1.3.  Composite Design Philosophy

322	   Composite algorithms, as defined in this specification, follow the
323	   definition in [RFC9794] and should be regarded as a single algorithm
324	   that performs a single cryptographic operation typical of a key
325	   establishment mechanism.  This generally means that the complexity of
326	   combining algorithms can and should be handled by the cryptographic
327	   library or cryptographic module.  The design intent is that protocols
328	   such as PKCS#10 [RFC2986], CMP [RFC9810], X.509 [RFC5280], the CMS
329	   [RFC5652], and the Trust Anchor Format [RFC5914] can treat composite
330	   algorithms as they would any other algorithm without the protocol
331	   layer to have any "hybrid-awareness".  This is a property referred to
332	   as "protocol backwards-compatibility".

334	   Discussion of the specific choices of algorithm pairings can be found
335	   in Section 6.2.

337	2.  Overview of the Composite ML-KEM Scheme

339	   Composite ML-KEM is a PQ/T hybrid Key Encapsulation Mechanism (KEM)
340	   which combines ML-KEM as specified in [FIPS.203] and [RFC9935] with
341	   one of RSA-OAEP defined in [RFC8017], the Elliptic Curve Diffie-
342	   Hellman key agreement schemes ECDH defined in section 5.7.1.2 of
343	   [SP.800-56Ar3], and X25519 / X448 defined in [RFC8410].  A KEM
344	   combiner function is used to combine the two component shared secret
345	   keys into a single shared secret key.

347	   Composite Key Encapsulation Mechanisms are defined as cryptographic
348	   primitives that consist of three algorithms.  These definitions are
349	   borrowed from [RFC9180].

351	   *  KeyGen() -> (pk, sk): A probabilistic key generation algorithm,
352	      which generates a public key pk and a secret key sk.  Some
353	      cryptographic modules may also expose a KeyGen(seed) -> (pk, sk),
354	      which generates pk and sk deterministically from a seed.  This
355	      specification assumes a seed-based keygen for ML-KEM.

357	   *  Encaps(pk) -> (ss, ct): A probabilistic encapsulation algorithm,
358	      which takes as input a public key pk and outputs a ciphertext ct
359	      and shared secret key ss.  Note: this specification uses Encaps()
360	      to conform to [FIPS.203], while [RFC9180] uses Encap().

362	   *  Decaps(sk, ct) -> ss: A decapsulation algorithm, which takes as
363	      input a secret key sk and ciphertext ct and outputs a shared
364	      secret ss.  Different KEM algorithms differ in how they handle
365	      decapsulation errors; some will return an error code ("explicit
366	      rejection"),while others will return a pseudorandomized shared
367	      secret key to mask from an attacker than an error occurred
368	      ("implicit rejection").  Note: this specification uses Decaps() to
369	      match [FIPS.203], while [RFC9180] uses Decap().

371	   The KEM interface was chosen as the interface for a composite key
372	   establishment because it allows for arbitrary combinations of
373	   component algorithm types since both key transport and key agreement
374	   mechanisms can be promoted into KEMs as described in Section 2.1 and
375	   Section 2.2 below.

377	   The following algorithms are defined for serializing and
378	   deserializing component values.  These algorithms are inspired by
379	   similar algorithms in [RFC9180].

381	   *  SerializePublicKey(mlkemPK, tradPK) -> bytes: Produce a byte
382	      string encoding of the component public keys.

384	   *  DeserializePublicKey(bytes) -> (mlkemPK, tradPK): Parse a byte
385	      string to recover the component public keys.

387	   *  SerializeCiphertext(mlkemCT, tradCT) -> bytes: Produce a byte
388	      string encoding of the component ciphertexts.

390	   *  DeserializeCiphertext(bytes) -> (mlkemCT, tradCT): Parse a byte
391	      string to recover the component ciphertexts.

393	   *  SerializePrivateKey(mlkemSeed, tradSK) -> bytes: Produce a byte
394	      string encoding of the component private keys.

396	   *  DeserializePrivateKey(bytes) -> (mlkemSeed, tradSK): Parse a byte
397	      string to recover the component private keys.

399	   Full definitions of serialization and deserialization algorithms can
400	   be found in Section 4.

402	2.1.  Promotion of RSA-OAEP into a KEM

404	   The RSA Optimal Asymmetric Encryption Padding (OAEP), as defined in
405	   section 7.1 of [RFC8017] is a public key encryption algorithm used to
406	   transport key material from a sender to a receiver.  A "key
407	   transport" type algorithm has the following API:

409	   *  Encrypt(pk, ss) -> ct: Take an existing shared secret key ss and
410	      encrypt it for pk.

412	   *  Decrypt(sk, ct) -> ss: Decrypt the ciphertext ct to recover ss.

414	   Note the difference between the API of RSA.Encrypt(pk, ss) -> ct and
415	   KEM.Encaps(pk) -> (ss, ct) presented above.  For this reason, RSA-
416	   OAEP cannot be directly combined with ML-KEM.  Fortunately, a key
417	   transport mechanism such as RSA-OAEP can be easily promoted into a
418	   KEM by having the sender generate a random 256 bit shared secret key
419	   and encrypt it.

421	   RSAOAEPKEM.Encaps(pkR):
422	     shared_secret = SecureRandom(ss_len)
423	     enc = RSAES-OAEP-ENCRYPT(pkR, shared_secret)

425	     return shared_secret, enc

427	   Note that the OAEP label L is left to its default value, which is the
428	   empty string as per [RFC8017].  The shared secret key output by the
429	   overall Composite ML-KEM already binds a composite KEM Combiner
430	   Label, so there is no need to also use the component Label.

432	   The value of ss_len as well as concrete values for all the RSA-OAEP
433	   parameters used within this specification can be found in
434	   Section 6.1.

436	   Decaps(sk, ct) -> ss is accomplished by direct use of OAEP Decrypt.

438	   RSAOAEPKEM.Decaps(skR, enc):
439	     shared_secret = RSAES-OAEP-DECRYPT(skR, enc)

441	     return shared_secret

443	   The encodings for the public key (pkR), private key (skR), and
444	   ciphertext (enc) are described in Section 4.

446	   A quick note on the choice of RSA-OAEP as the supported RSA
447	   encryption primitive.  RSA-KEM [RFC9690] is cryptographically robust
448	   and is more straightforward to work with, but it has fairly limited
449	   adoption and therefore is of limited value as a PQ migration
450	   mechanism.  Also, while RSA-PKCS#1v1.5 [RFC8017] is still widely
451	   used, it is hard to make secure and no longer FIPS-approved as of the
452	   end of 2023 [SP800-131Ar2], so it is of limited forwards value.  This
453	   leaves RSA-OAEP [RFC8017] as the remaining choice.  See Section 6.2
454	   for further discussion of algorithm choices.

456	   Note that, at least at the time of writing, the algorithm RSAOAEPKEM
457	   is not defined as a standalone algorithm within PKIX standards and it
458	   does not have an assigned algorithm OID, so it cannot be used
459	   directly with CMS KEMRecipientInfo [RFC9629]; it is merely a building
460	   block for the composite algorithm.

462	2.2.  Promotion of ECDH into a KEM

464	   A "key agreement" or "Diffie-Hellman (DH)" type algorithm is a key
465	   establishment algorithm requiring both parties to contribute an
466	   asymmetric keypair to the derivation of the shared secret key.  DH
467	   algorithms have the following API:

469	   *  DH(skX, pkY) -> ss: Each party combines their secret key skX with
470	      the other party's public key pkY.

472	   In this specification, we consider only the elliptic curve Diffie-
473	   Hellman algorithm identified by the OID id-ecDH as defined in
474	   [RFC5480] and [SEC1].

476	   Note the difference between the API of DH(skX, pkY) -> ss and
477	   KEM.Encaps(pk) -> (ss, ct) presented above.  For this reason, a
478	   Diffie-Hellman key exchange cannot be directly combined with ML-KEM.
479	   Fortunately, a Diffie-Hellman key agreement can be easily promoted
480	   into a KEM.Encaps(pk) -> (ss, ct) by having the sender generate an
481	   ephemeral keypair for themself and sending their public key as the
482	   ciphertext ct.  Composite ML-KEM uses a simplified version of the
483	   DHKEM definition from [RFC9180]:

485	   DHKEM.Encaps(pkR):
486	     (skE, pkE) = GenerateKeyPair()
487	     ss = DH(skE, pkR)
488	     ct = SerializePublicKey(pkE)

490	     return ss, ct

492	   Decaps(sk, ct) -> ss is accomplished in the analogous way.

494	   DHKEM.Decaps(skR, ct):
495	     pkE = DeserializePublicKey(ct)
496	     ss = DH(skR, pkE)

498	     return ss

500	   This construction applies for all variants of elliptic curve Diffie-
501	   Hellman used in this specification: ECDH, X25519, and X448.

503	   For ECDH, DH() yields the value Z as described in section 5.7.1.2 of
504	   [SP.800-56Ar3].

506	   For X25519 and X448, DH() yields the value K as described in section
507	   6 of [RFC7748].

509	   The encodings for the public key (pkR), private key (skR), and
510	   ciphertext (pkE) are described in Section 4.

512	   The promotion of DH to a KEM is similar to the DHKEM functions in
513	   [RFC9180], but it is simplified in the following ways:

515	   1.  Notation has been aligned to the notation used in this
516	       specification.

518	   2.  Since a KEM Combiner Label is included explicitly in the
519	       Composite ML-KEM combiner, there is no need to perform the
520	       labeled steps of ExtractAndExpand().

522	   3.  Since the ciphertext and receiver's public key are included
523	       explicitly in the Composite ML-KEM combiner, there is no need to
524	       construct the kem_context object.

526	   Note that here, SerializePublicKey() and DeserializePublicKey() refer
527	   to the underlying encoding of the DH primitive, and not to the
528	   composite serialization functions defined in Section 4.

530	   Note that, at least at the time of writing, the algorithm DHKEM is
531	   not defined as a standalone algorithm within PKIX standards and it
532	   does not have an assigned algorithm OID, so it cannot be used
533	   directly with CMS KEMRecipientInfo [RFC9629]; it is merely a building
534	   block for the composite algorithm.

536	3.  Composite ML-KEM Functions

538	   This section describes the composite ML-KEM functions needed to
539	   instantiate the public API of a Key Encapsulation Mechanism as
540	   defined in Section 2.

542	3.1.  Key Generation

544	   In order to maintain security properties of the composite, this
545	   specification strictly forbids re-using component key material
546	   between composite and non-composite keys, or between multiple
547	   composite keys.  This means that an invocation of Composite-ML-
548	   KEM.KeyGen() MUST perform, or otherwise guarantee, fresh generation
549	   of the key material for both underlying algorithms and MUST NOT reuse
550	   existing key material.  See Section 9.3 for a discussion.

552	   To generate a new keypair for composite schemes, the KeyGen() -> (pk,
553	   sk) function is used.  The KeyGen() function calls the two key
554	   generation functions of the component algorithms independently.
555	   Multi-threaded, multi-process, or multi-module applications might
556	   choose to execute the key generation functions in parallel for better
557	   key generation performance or architectural modularity.

559	   To generate an ML-KEM key pair this specification uses the function
560	   ML-KEM.KeyGen_internal(d, z).  According to [FIPS.203] d and z are
561	   two random 32 Byte values.  This document combines both values in
562	   mlkemSeed by concatenating them so that mlkemSeed = d || z.

564	   The following describes how to instantiate a KeyGen() function for a
565	   given composite algorithm represented by <OID>.

567	   Composite-ML-KEM<OID>.KeyGen() -> (pk, sk)

569	   Explicit Inputs:
570	        None

572	   Implicit Inputs mapped from <OID>:

574	     ML-KEM     The underlying ML-KEM algorithm and
575	                parameter set, for example "ML-KEM-768".

577	     Trad       The underlying traditional algorithm and
578	                parameter, for example "RSA-OAEP"
579	                or "X25519".

581	   Output:
582	     (pk, sk)  The composite keypair.

584	   Key Generation Process:

586	     1. Generate component keys

588	       mlkemSeed = Random(64)
589	       (mlkemPK, mlkemSK) = ML-KEM.KeyGen_internal(
590	                                         mlkemSeed[:32],
591	                                         mlkemSeed[32:] )
592	       (tradPK, tradSK) = Trad.KeyGen()

594	     2. Check for component key gen failure
595	       if NOT (mlkemPK, mlkemSK) or NOT (tradPK, tradSK):
596	         output "Key generation error"

598	     3. Output the composite public and private keys

600	       pk = SerializePublicKey(mlkemPK, tradPK)
601	       sk = SerializePrivateKey(mlkemSeed, tradSK)
602	       return (pk, sk)

604	   In order to ensure fresh keys, the key generation functions MUST be
605	   executed for both component algorithms.  Compliant parties MUST NOT
606	   use, import or export component keys that are used in other contexts,
607	   combinations, or by themselves as keys for standalone algorithm use.
608	   For more details on the security considerations around key reuse, see
609	   Section 9.3.

611	   If one of the component KeyGen() routines returns an error, then this
612	   error MUST be propagated by the Composite-ML-KEM.KeyGen() routine.
613	   Further discussion can be found below in Section 3.5.

615	3.1.1.  Allowed Modifications to the Key Generation Process

617	   Key generation is a process that is entirely internal to a
618	   cryptographic module, and as such it is often customized to fit the
619	   performance or operational requirements of the module.  In cases
620	   where the private keys never leave the module or are otherwise not
621	   required to interoperate with other cryptographic modules, it is not
622	   required for interoperability for the private keys to match the
623	   format described in this specification.  Therefore, in general,
624	   implementations of Composite ML-KEM MAY use an alternate key
625	   generation process so long as it generates compatible public keys,
626	   and so long as both component keys are freshly-generated and not re-
627	   used in a standalone key or within another composite key.  Below are
628	   some examples of modifications that an implementer MAY make to the
629	   key generation process.

631	   Implementations MAY modify this process to additionally output the
632	   expanded mlkemSK or to make use of ML-KEM.KeyGen_internal(d, z) as
633	   needed to expand the ML-KEM seed (d || z) into an expanded key prior
634	   to performing a signing operation.

636	   In cases where it is desirable to have a deterministic KeyGen of one
637	   or both component keys from a seed, this process MAY be modified to
638	   expose an interface of Composite-ML-KEM<OID>.KeyGen(seed) such that
639	   one component algorithm is generated from the seed and the other from
640	   random, or the input seed is cryptographically expanded to produce
641	   seeds for both components.  Security analysis of such a modified key
642	   generation process is outside the scope of this document.

644	   Where interoperable private keys are not required, implementations
645	   MAY choose to use a different private key representation than the one
646	   given in Section 4.2.  For example, the component keys MAY be stored
647	   in separate cryptographic modules, or MAY be stored in separate
648	   PKCS#8 objects, or MAY be stored in a format that preserves the ML-
649	   KEM expanded key instead of the ML-KEM seed.  The required
650	   modifications to the key generation process, as well as the signature
651	   generation process below, to support these private key
652	   representations are considered compliant with this specification so
653	   long as they generate compatible public keys, and so long as both
654	   component keys are freshly-generated.  Note that when implementing
655	   Composite ML-KEM with a private key format that does not preserve the
656	   ML-KEM seed, especially when implementing on top of a cryptographic
657	   module that does not support seeds, it will be impossible to
658	   reconstruct a compliant seed-based private key as described in
659	   Section 4.2

661	3.2.  Encapsulation

663	   The Encaps(pk) of a Composite ML-KEM algorithm is designed to behave
664	   exactly the same as ML-KEM.Encaps(ek) defined in Algorithm 20 in
665	   Section 7.2 of [FIPS.203].  Specifically, Composite-ML-KEM.Encaps(pk)
666	   produces a 256-bit shared secret key that can be used directly with
667	   any symmetric-key cryptographic algorithm.  In this way, Composite
668	   ML-KEM can be used as a direct drop-in replacement anywhere that ML-
669	   KEM is used.

671	   The following describes how to instantiate a Encaps(pk) function for
672	   a given composite algorithm represented by <OID>.

674	   Composite-ML-KEM<OID>.Encaps(pk) -> (ss, ct)

676	   Explicit Inputs:

678	     pk      Composite public key consisting of encryption public keys
679	             for each component.

681	   Implicit inputs mapped from <OID>:

683	     ML-KEM  The underlying ML-KEM algorithm and
684	             parameter set, for example "ML-KEM-768".

686	     Trad    The underlying ML-KEM algorithm and
687	             parameter set, for example "RSA-OAEP"
688	             or "X25519".

690	     Label   KEM Combiner Label value for binding the ciphertext to the
691	             Composite OID. Label values are defined per composite
692	             algorithm.

694	   Output:

696	     ss      The shared secret key, a 256-bit key suitable for use with
697	             symmetric cryptographic algorithms.

699	     ct      The ciphertext, a byte string.

701	   Encap Process:

703	     1. Separate the public keys.

705	         (mlkemPK, tradPK) = DeserializePublicKey(pk)

707	     2.  Perform the respective component Encap operations according to
708	         their algorithm specifications.

710	         (mlkemCT, mlkemSS) = ML-KEM.Encaps(mlkemPK)
711	         (tradCT, tradSS) = TradKEM.Encaps(tradPK)

713	     3. If either ML-KEM.Encaps() or TradKEM.Encaps() return an error,
714	        then this process must return an error.

716	         if NOT (mlkemCT, mlkemSS) or NOT (tradCT, tradSS):
717	           output "Encapsulation error"

719	     4. Encode the ciphertext

721	         ct = SerializeCiphertext(mlkemCT, tradCT)

723	     5. Combine the KEM secrets and additional context to yield the
724	        composite shared secret key.

726	           ss = KemCombiner(mlkemSS, tradSS, tradCT, tradPK, Label)

728	     6. Output composite shared secret key and ciphertext.

730	        return (ss, ct)

732	   The specific values for OID and Label are defined per Composite ML-
733	   KEM algorithm in Section 6.

735	   Errors produced by the component Encaps() routines MUST be forwarded
736	   on to the calling application.  Further discussion can be found below
737	   in Section 3.5.

739	3.3.  Decapsulation

741	   The Decaps(sk, ct) -> ss of a Composite ML-KEM algorithm is designed
742	   to behave exactly the same as ML-KEM.Decaps(dk, c) defined in
743	   Algorithm 21 in Section 7.3 of [FIPS.203].  Specifically, Composite-
744	   ML-KEM.Decaps(sk, ct) produces a 256-bit shared secret key that can
745	   be used directly with any symmetric-key cryptographic algorithm.  In
746	   this way, Composite ML-KEM can be used as a direct drop-in
747	   replacement anywhere that ML-KEM is used.

749	   The following describes how to instantiate a Decaps(sk, ct) function
750	   for a given composite algorithm represented by <OID>.

752	   Composite-ML-KEM<OID>.Decaps(sk, ct) -> ss

754	   Explicit inputs

756	     sk      Composite private key consisting of decryption private
757	             keys for each component.

759	     ct      The ciphertext, a byte string.

761	   Implicit inputs mapped from <OID>:

763	     ML-KEM  The underlying ML-KEM algorithm and
764	             parameter set, for example "ML-KEM-768".

766	     Trad    The underlying traditional algorithm and
767	             parameter set, for example "RSA-OAEP"
768	             or "X25519".

770	     Label   KEM Combiner Label value for binding the ciphertext to the
771	             Composite OID. Label values are defined per composite
772	             algorithm.

774	   Implicit inputs looked up from SK:

776	     tradPK  The traditional public key is required for the KEM
777	             combiner.
778	             For discussion of where to get this value, see the
779	             Operational Consideration section 10.4:
780	             "Decapsulation Requires the Public Key" for more discussion
781	             on this point.

783	   Output:

785	     ss      The shared secret key, a 256-bit key suitable for use
786	             with symmetric cryptographic algorithms.

788	   Decap Process:

790	     1. Separate the private keys and ciphertexts

792	         (mlkemSeed, tradSK) = DeserializePrivateKey(sk)
793	         (_, mlkemSK) = ML-KEM.KeyGen(mlkemSeed[:32], mlkemSeed[32:])
794	         (mlkemCT, tradCT) = DeserializeCiphertext(ct)

796	     2.  Perform the respective component Decap operations according
797	         to their algorithm specifications.

799	         mlkemSS = ML-KEM.Decaps(mlkemSK, mlkemCT)
800	         tradSS  = TradKEM.Decaps(tradSK, tradCT)

802	     3. If either ML-KEM.Decaps() or TradKEM.Decaps() return an error,
803	        then this process must return an error.

805	         if NOT mlkemSS or NOT tradSS:
806	           output "Decapsulation error"

808	     4. Combine the KEM secrets and additional context to yield the
809	        composite shared secret key.

811	         ss = KemCombiner(mlkemSS, tradSS, tradCT, tradPK, Label)

813	     5. Output composite shared secret key.

815	        return ss

817	   The specific values for OID and Label are defined per Composite ML-
818	   KEM algorithm in Section 6.

820	   Steps 2 and 4 SHOULD be performed in a timing-invariant way to
821	   prevent side-channel attackers from learning any of the inputs or
822	   output of the KEM combiner.

824	   Step 4 requires the Decaps() process to have access to tradPK, which
825	   is not carried in the private key format and therefore the
826	   implementation is required to acquire it from some out-of-band means.
827	   The Operational Considerations Section 10.4 provides further
828	   discussion on this.

830	   It is possible to use component private keys stored in separate
831	   software or hardware keystores.  Variations in the process to
832	   accommodate particular private key storage mechanisms are considered
833	   to be conformant to this specification so long as it produces the
834	   same output and error handling as the process sketched above.

836	   In order to properly achieve its security properties, the KEM
837	   combiner requires that all inputs are fixed-length or length-encoded.
838	   Since each Composite ML-KEM algorithm fully specifies its component
839	   algorithms, including key sizes, all inputs should be fixed-length in
840	   non-error scenarios except for minor variations introduced by
841	   encoding.  In the cases where there are minor variations introduced
842	   by encoding, those encodings already have a fixed-length prefix
843	   followed by length-encoded data, so the requirements for the KEM
844	   combiner security properties hold (namely that the input is
845	   injective).  However some implementations may choose to perform
846	   additional checking to handle certain error conditions.  In
847	   particular, the KEM combiner step should not be performed if either
848	   of the component decapsulations returned an error condition
849	   indicating malformed inputs.  RSA-based composites MUST ensure that
850	   the modulus size (i.e. the size of tradCT and tradPK) matches that
851	   specified for the given Composite ML-KEM algorithm in Section 6;
852	   depending on the cryptographic library used, this check may be done
853	   by the library or may require an explicit check as part of the
854	   Composite-ML-KEM.Decaps() routine.  Implementers should keep in mind
855	   that some instances of tradCT and tradPK will be DER-encoded which
856	   could introduce minor length variations such as dropping leading
857	   zeroes; since the underlying KEMs are assumed to be IND-CCA secure,
858	   decapsulation against tampered ciphertexts or public keys is assumed
859	   to fail, these length differences are considered benign to the KEM
860	   combiner.

862	   Errors produced by the component Decaps() routines MUST be forwarded
863	   on to the calling application.  Further discussion can be found below
864	   in Section 3.5.

866	3.4.  KEM Combiner Function

868	   This specification provides a combiner construction with SHA3-256 for
869	   all combinations of algorithms.

871	   KemCombiner(mlkemSS, tradSS, tradCT, tradPK, Label) -> ss

873	   Explicit inputs:

875	     The list of input values to be combined.

877	   Output:

879	     ss      The shared secret key, a 256-bit key suitable for use with
880	             symmetric cryptographic algorithms.

882	   Process:

884	     ss = SHA3-256(mlkemSS || tradSS || tradCT || tradPK || Label)

886	     return ss

888	3.5.  Error Handling and Explicit Rejection

890	   ML-KEM, particularly its Decaps() defined in Algorithms 18 and 21 of
891	   [FIPS.203], is designed to be implicitly rejecting, meaning that a
892	   failure within the underlying PKE scheme due to a mangled ciphertext
893	   will not cause ML-KEM.Decaps() to return an error, but instead any
894	   errors encountered during decapsulation are handled by producing a
895	   pseudo-random shared secret.  ML-KEM.Decaps() can, however return
896	   errors for example if the provided ciphertext or decapsulation
897	   private key is the wrong size.

899	   In Composite ML-KEM, not all component algorithms will be implicitly
900	   rejecting, for example RSA-OAEP's Decrypt() can return an error if
901	   the padding is incorrect.  In general, in the case that one of the
902	   component primitives generates an error during Composite ML-KEM
903	   KeyGen, Encaps, or Decaps, Composite ML-KEM MUST clear all buffers
904	   containing key material and forward the error to its caller; i.e.
905	   Composite ML-KEM MUST be explicitly rejecting whenever one of its
906	   components is.  The same applies to Composite ML-KEM KeyGen() and
907	   Encaps(): Composite KEM MUST forward any errors produced by component
908	   algorithms.

910	4.  Serialization

912	   This section presents routines for serializing and deserializing
913	   composite public keys, private keys, and ciphertext values to bytes.
914	   The functions defined in this section are considered internal
915	   implementation details and are referenced from within the public API
916	   definitions in Section 3.

918	   Deserialization is possible because ML-KEM has fixed-length public
919	   keys, private keys (seeds), and ciphertext values as shown in the
920	   following table, which is similar to Table 3 from [FIPS.203], but
921	   with a different private key representation.

923	          +=============+============+=============+============+
924	          | Algorithm   | Public Key | Private Key | Ciphertext |
925	          +=============+============+=============+============+
926	          | ML-KEM-768  | 1184       | 64          | 1088       |
927	          +-------------+------------+-------------+------------+
928	          | ML-KEM-1024 | 1568       | 64          | 1568       |
929	          +-------------+------------+-------------+------------+

931	                       Table 1: ML-KEM Sizes in bytes

933	   For all serialization routines below, when these values are required
934	   to be carried in an ASN.1 structure, they are wrapped as described in
935	   Section 5.1.

937	   While ML-KEM has a single fixed-size representation for each of
938	   public key, private key, and ciphertext, the traditional component
939	   might allow multiple valid encodings; for example an elliptic curve
940	   public key, and therefore also ciphertext, might be validly encoded
941	   as either compressed or uncompressed [SEC1], or an RSA private key
942	   could be encoded in Chinese Remainder Theorem form [RFC8017].  In
943	   order to obtain interoperability, composite algorithms MUST use the
944	   following encodings of the underlying components:

946	   *  *ML-KEM*: MUST be encoded as specified in sections 7.1 and 7.2 of
947	      [FIPS.203], using a 64-byte seed (d || z) as the private key.

949	   *  *RSA*: the public key MUST be encoded as RSAPublicKey with the
950	      (n,e) public key representation as specified in A.1.1 of [RFC8017]
951	      and the private key representation as RSAPrivateKey specified in
952	      A.1.2 of [RFC8017] with version 0 and 'otherPrimeInfos' absent.
953	      An RSA-OAEP ciphertext MUST be encoded as specified in section
954	      7.1.1 of [RFC8017]

956	   *  *ECDH*: public key MUST be encoded as an uncompressed elliptic
957	      curve point as in section 2.2 of [RFC5480], including the leading
958	      byte 0x04 indicating uncompressed encoding and without the ASN.1
959	      OCTET STRING wrapper.  This is consistent with the encoding of EC
960	      public keys in X9.62 [X9.62_2005].  The private key MUST be
961	      encoded as ECPrivateKey specified in [RFC5915] with 'NamedCurve'
962	      parameter set to the OID of the curve, but without the 'publicKey'
963	      field.  The ciphertext MUST be encoded in the same manner as the
964	      public key.

966	   *  *X25519 and X448*: the public key MUST be encoded as per section 5
967	      of [RFC7748] and the private key is a 32 or 56 byte raw value for
968	      X25519 and X448 respectively.  The ciphertext MUST be encoded in
969	      the same manner as the public key.

971	   All ASN.1 objects SHALL be encoded using DER on serialization.

973	   Even with fixed encodings for the traditional component, there may be
974	   slight differences in size of the encoded value due to, for example,
975	   encoding rules that drop leading zeroes.  See Appendix A for further
976	   discussion of encoded size of each composite algorithm.

978	   The deserialization routines described below do not check for well-
979	   formedness of the cryptographic material they are recovering.  It is
980	   assumed that underlying cryptographic primitives will catch malformed
981	   values and raise an appropriate error.

983	4.1.  SerializePublicKey and DeserializePublicKey

985	   The serialization routine for keys simply concatenates the public
986	   keys of the component algorithms, as defined below:

988	   Composite-ML-KEM.SerializePublicKey(mlkemPK, tradPK) -> bytes

990	   Explicit inputs:

992	     mlkemPK The ML-KEM public key, which is bytes.

994	     tradPK  The traditional public key in the appropriate
995	             encoding for the underlying component algorithm.

997	   Implicit inputs:

999	     None

1001	   Output:

1003	     bytes   The encoded composite public key.

1005	   Serialization Process:

1007	     1. Combine and output the encoded public key

1009	        output mlkemPK || tradPK

1011	   Deserialization reverses this process.  Each component key is
1012	   deserialized according to their respective specification as shown in
1013	   Appendix B.

1015	   The following describes how to instantiate a
1016	   DeserializePublicKey(bytes) function for a given composite algorithm
1017	   represented by <OID>.

1019	   Composite-ML-KEM<OID>.DeserializePublicKey(bytes) ->(mlkemPK, tradPK)

1021	   Explicit inputs:

1023	     bytes   An encoded composite public key.

1025	   Implicit inputs mapped from <OID>:

1027	     ML-KEM   The underlying ML-KEM algorithm and
1028	              parameter, for example, could be "ML-KEM-768".

1030	   Output:

1032	     mlkemPK  The ML-KEM public key, which is bytes.

1034	     tradPK   The traditional public key in the appropriate
1035	              encoding for the underlying component algorithm.

1037	   Deserialization Process:

1039	     1. Parse each constituent encoded public key.
1040	          The length of the mlkemPK is known based on the size of
1041	          the ML-KEM component key length specified by the Object ID.

1043	        switch ML-KEM do
1044	           case ML-KEM-768:
1045	             mlkemPK = bytes[:1184]
1046	             tradPK  = bytes[1184:]
1047	           case ML-KEM-1024:
1048	             mlkemPK = bytes[:1568]
1049	             tradPK  = bytes[1568:]

1051	        Note that while ML-KEM has fixed-length keys, RSA
1052	        may not, depending on encoding, so rigorous length-checking
1053	        of the overall composite key is not always possible.

1055	     2. Output the component public keys

1057	        output (mlkemPK, tradPK)

1059	4.2.  SerializePrivateKey and DeserializePrivateKey

1061	   The serialization routine for keys simply concatenates the private
1062	   keys of the component algorithms, as defined below:

1064	   Composite-ML-KEM.SerializePrivateKey(mlkemSeed, tradSK) -> bytes

1066	   Explicit inputs:

1068	     mlkemSeed  The ML-KEM private key, which consists of a 32 Byte seed
1069	                value d concatenated with a 32 Byte seed value z.

1071	     tradSK     The traditional private key in the appropriate
1072	                encoding for the underlying component algorithm.

1074	   Implicit inputs:

1076	     None

1078	   Output:

1080	     bytes   The encoded composite private key.

1082	   Serialization Process:

1084	     1. Combine and output the encoded private key.

1086	        output mlkemSeed || tradSK

1088	   Deserialization reverses this process.  Each component key is
1089	   deserialized according to their respective specification as shown in
1090	   Appendix B.

1092	   The following describes how to instantiate a
1093	   DeserializePrivateKey(bytes) function.  Since ML-KEM private keys are
1094	   64 bytes for all parameter sets, this function does not need to be
1095	   parametrized.

1097	   Composite-ML-KEM.DeserializePrivateKey(bytes)
1098	                                       -> (mlkemSeed, tradSK)

1100	   Explicit inputs:

1102	     bytes   An encoded composite private key.

1104	   Implicit inputs:

1106	     That an ML-KEM private key is 64 bytes for all parameter sets.

1108	   Output:

1110	     mlkemSeed  The ML-KEM private key, which consists of a 32 Byte seed
1111	                value d concatenated with a 32 Byte seed value z.

1113	     tradSK    The traditional private key in the appropriate
1114	                encoding for the underlying component algorithm.

1116	   Deserialization Process:

1118	     1. Parse the ML-KEM seed, which is always a 64 byte seed
1119	        for all parameter sets.

1121	        mlkemSeed = bytes[:64]
1122	        tradSK    = bytes[64:]

1124	     2. Output the component private keys

1126	        output (mlkemSeed,tradSK)

1128	4.3.  SerializeCiphertext and DeserializeCiphertext

1130	   The serialization routine for the composite ciphertext value simply
1131	   concatenates the fixed-length ML-KEM ciphertext with the ciphertext
1132	   from the traditional algorithm, as defined below:

1134	   Composite-ML-KEM.SerializeCiphertext(mlkemCT, tradCT) -> bytes

1136	   Explicit inputs:

1138	     mlkemCT  The ML-KEM ciphertext, which is bytes.

1140	     tradCT   The traditional ciphertext in the appropriate
1141	              encoding for the underlying component algorithm.

1143	   Implicit inputs:

1145	     None

1147	   Output:

1149	     bytes   The encoded composite ciphertext value.

1151	   Serialization Process:

1153	     1. Combine and output the encoded composite ciphertext

1155	        output mlkemCT || tradCT

1157	   Deserialization reverses this process.  Each component ciphertext is
1158	   deserialized according to their respective specification as shown in
1159	   Appendix B.

1161	   The following describes how to instantiate a
1162	   DeserializeCiphertext(bytes) function for a given composite algorithm
1163	   represented by <OID>.

1165	   Composite-ML-KEM<OID>.DeserializeCiphertext(bytes)
1166	                                             -> (mlkemCT, tradCT)

1168	   Explicit inputs:

1170	     bytes   An encoded composite ciphertext value.

1172	   Implicit inputs mapped from <OID>:

1174	     ML-KEM   The underlying ML-KEM algorithm and
1175	              parameter, for example, could be "ML-KEM-768".

1177	   Output:

1179	     mlkemCT  The ML-KEM ciphertext, which is bytes.

1181	     tradCT   The traditional ciphertext in the appropriate
1182	              encoding for the underlying component algorithm.

1184	   Deserialization Process:

1186	     1. Parse each constituent encoded ciphertext.
1187	        The length of the mlkemCT is known based on the size of the
1188	        ML-KEM component ciphertext length specified by the Object ID.

1190	        switch ML-KEM do
1191	           case ML-KEM-768:
1192	             mlkemCT = bytes[:1088]
1193	             tradCT  = bytes[1088:]
1194	           case ML-KEM-1024:
1195	             mlkemCT= bytes[:1568]
1196	             tradCT  = bytes[1568:]

1198	     2. Output the component ciphertext values

1200	        output (mlkemCT, tradCT)

1202	5.  Use within X.509 and PKIX

1204	   The following sections provide processing logic and the necessary
1205	   ASN.1 modules necessary to use composite ML-KEM within X.509 and PKIX
1206	   protocols.  Use within the Cryptographic Message Syntax (CMS) will be
1207	   covered in a separate specification.

1209	   While composite ML-KEM keys and ciphertext values MAY be used raw,
1210	   the following sections provide conventions for using them within
1211	   X.509 and other PKIX protocols such that Composite ML-KEM can be used
1212	   as a drop-in replacement for KEM algorithms in PKCS#10 [RFC2986], CMP
1213	   [RFC9810], X.509 [RFC5280], and related protocols.

1215	5.1.  Encoding to DER

1217	   The serialization routines presented in Section 4 produce raw binary
1218	   values.  When these values are required to be carried within a DER-
1219	   encoded message format such as an X.509's subjectPublicKey or a
1220	   OneAsymmetricKey.privateKey OCTET STRING [RFC5958], then the BIT
1221	   STRING or OCTET STRING contains this raw byte string output of the
1222	   appropriate serialization routine from Section 4 without further
1223	   encoding.

1225	   When a Composite ML-KEM public key appears outside of a
1226	   SubjectPublicKeyInfo type in an environment that uses ASN.1 encoding,
1227	   it could be encoded as an OCTET STRING by using the Composite-ML-KEM-
1228	   PublicKey type defined below.

1230	   Composite-ML-KEM-PublicKey ::= OCTET STRING

1232	   Size constraints MAY be enforced, as appropriate as per Appendix A.

1234	5.2.  Key Usage Bits

1236	   When any Composite ML-KEM Object Identifier appears within the
1237	   SubjectPublicKeyInfo.AlgorithmIdentifier field of an X.509
1238	   certificate [RFC5280], the key usage certificate extension MUST only
1239	   contain:

1241	   keyEncipherment

1243	   Composite ML-KEM keys MUST NOT be used in a "dual usage" mode because
1244	   even if the traditional component key supports both signing and
1245	   encryption, the post-quantum algorithms do not and therefore the
1246	   overall composite algorithm does not.  Implementations MUST NOT use
1247	   one component of the composite for the purposes of digital signature
1248	   and the other component for the purposes of encryption or key
1249	   establishment.

1251	5.3.  ASN.1 Definitions

1253	   Composite ML-KEM uses a substantially non-ASN.1 based encoding, as
1254	   specified in Section 4.  However, as composite algorithms will be
1255	   used within ASN.1-based X.509 and PKIX protocols, some conventions
1256	   for ASN.1 wrapping are necessary.

1258	   The following ASN.1 Information Object Classes are defined to allow
1259	   for compact definitions of each composite algorithm, leading to a
1260	   smaller overall ASN.1 module.

1262	   pk-CompositeKEM {OBJECT IDENTIFIER:id}
1263	     PUBLIC-KEY ::= {
1264	       IDENTIFIER id
1265	       -- KEY no ASN.1 wrapping --
1266	       PARAMS ARE absent
1267	       CERT-KEY-USAGE { keyEncipherment }
1268	       -- PRIVATE-KEY no ASN.1 wrapping --
1269	     }

1271	   kema-CompositeKEM {
1272	     OBJECT IDENTIFIER:id,
1273	       PUBLIC-KEY:publicKeyType }
1274	       KEM-ALGORITHM ::= {
1275	            IDENTIFIER id
1276	            -- VALUE no ASN.1 wrapping --
1277	            PARAMS ARE absent
1278	            PUBLIC-KEYS { publicKeyType }
1279	            SMIME-CAPS { IDENTIFIED BY id }
1280	           }

1282	      Figure 1: ASN.1 Object Information Classes for Composite ML-KEM

1284	   As an example, the public key and KEM algorithm types associated with
1285	   id-MLKEM768-ECDH-P256-SHA3-256 are defined as:

1287	   pk-MLKEM768-ECDH-P256-SHA3-256 PUBLIC-KEY ::=
1288	     pk-CompositeKEM {
1289	       id-MLKEM768-ECDH-P256-SHA3-256 }

1291	   kema-MLKEM768-ECDH-P256-SHA3-256 KEM-ALGORITHM ::=
1292	       kema-CompositeKEM{
1293	         id-MLKEM768-ECDH-P256-SHA3-256,
1294	         pk-MLKEM768-ECDH-P256-SHA3-256 }

1296	   The full set of key types defined by this specification can be found
1297	   in the ASN.1 Module in Section 7.

1299	   Use cases that require an interoperable encoding for composite
1300	   private keys will often need to place a composite private key inside
1301	   a OneAsymmetricKey structure defined in [RFC5958], such as when
1302	   private keys are carried in PKCS #12 [RFC7292], CMP [RFC9810] or CRMF
1303	   [RFC4211].  The definition of OneAsymmetricKey is copied here for
1304	   convenience:

1306	    OneAsymmetricKey ::= SEQUENCE {
1307	          version                   Version,
1308	          privateKeyAlgorithm       PrivateKeyAlgorithmIdentifier,
1309	          privateKey                PrivateKey,
1310	          attributes            [0] Attributes OPTIONAL,
1311	          ...,
1312	          [[2: publicKey        [1] PublicKey OPTIONAL ]],
1313	          ...
1314	        }

1316	     ...
1317	     PrivateKey ::= OCTET STRING
1318	                         -- Content varies based on type of key.  The
1319	                         -- algorithm identifier dictates the format of
1320	                         -- the key.

1322	             Figure 2: OneAsymmetricKey as defined in [RFC5958]

1324	   When a composite private key is conveyed inside a OneAsymmetricKey
1325	   structure (version 1 of which is also known as PrivateKeyInfo)
1326	   [RFC5958], the privateKeyAlgorithm field SHALL be set to the
1327	   corresponding composite algorithm identifier defined according to
1328	   Section 6 and its parameters field MUST be absent.  The privateKey
1329	   field SHALL contain the OCTET STRING representation of the serialized
1330	   composite private key as per Section 4.2.  The publicKey field
1331	   remains OPTIONAL.  If the publicKey field is present, it MUST be a
1332	   composite public key as per Section 4.1.

1334	   Some applications might need to reconstruct the SubjectPublicKeyInfo
1335	   or OneAsymmetricKey objects corresponding to each component key
1336	   individually, for example if this is required for invoking the
1337	   underlying primitive.  Section 6 provides the necessary mapping
1338	   between composite and their component algorithms for doing this
1339	   reconstruction.

1341	   Component keys of a composite private key MUST NOT be used in any
1342	   other type of key or as a standalone key.  For more details on the
1343	   security considerations around key reuse, see Section 9.3.

1345	6.  Algorithm Identifiers and Parameters

1347	   This section lists the algorithm identifiers and parameters for all
1348	   Composite ML-KEM algorithms.

1350	   Full specifications for the referenced algorithms can be found in
1351	   Appendix B.

1353	   As the number of algorithms can be daunting to implementers, see
1354	   Section 10.3 for a discussion of choosing a subset to support.

1356	   Each Composite ML-KEM algorithm has a unique Label which is used in
1357	   constructing the KEM combiner in (Section 3.4).  This helps protect
1358	   against a different algorithm arriving at the same shared secret key
1359	   even if all inputs are the same.

1361	   Label values are provided in two forms: where the label value is
1362	   alphanumeric ASCII, they are represented below as strings.  For
1363	   example, "MLKEM768-RSAOAEP2048" below is equivalent to the
1364	   hexadecimal value 4D4C4B454D3736382D5253414F41455032303438.

1366	   Some of the label values contain problematic characters, such as
1367	   backslashes, that can cause issues displaying correctly in rendered
1368	   documents or even in source code when the compiler interprets it as
1369	   an escape character.  Below, these are represented directly in
1370	   hexadecimal.  For example, the label for id-MLKEM768-X25519-SHA3-256
1371	   is "\.//^\", but to avoid transcription errors it is provided only in
1372	   hexadecimal as 5c2e2f2f5e5c.

1374	   Composite KEM algorithm list:

1376	   *  id-MLKEM768-RSA2048-SHA3-256

1378	      -  OID: 1.3.6.1.5.5.7.6.55

1380	      -  Label: "MLKEM768-RSAOAEP2048"

1382	      -  ML-KEM variant: ML-KEM-768

1384	      -  Traditional Algorithm: RSA

1386	         o  Traditional KEM Algorithm: id-RSAES-OAEP

1388	         o  RSA size: 2048

1390	         o  RSAES-OAEP parameters: See Table 2

1392	   *  id-MLKEM768-RSA3072-SHA3-256

1398	      -  ML-KEM variant: ML-KEM-768

1400	      -  Traditional Algorithm: RSA
1401	         o  Traditional KEM Algorithm: id-RSAES-OAEP

1403	         o  RSA size: 3072

1405	         o  RSAES-OAEP parameters: See Table 2

1407	   *  id-MLKEM768-RSA4096-SHA3-256

1409	      -  OID: 1.3.6.1.5.5.7.6.57

1413	      -  ML-KEM variant: ML-KEM-768

1471	      -  Traditional Algorithm: ECDH

1475	         o  ECDH curve: brainpoolP256r1

1477	   *  id-MLKEM1024-RSA3072-SHA3-256

1479	      -  OID: 1.3.6.1.5.5.7.6.62

1481	      -  Label: "MLKEM1024-RSAOAEP3072"

1483	      -  ML-KEM variant: ML-KEM-1024

1485	      -  Traditional Algorithm: RSA

1489	         o  RSA size: 3072

1491	         o  RSAES-OAEP parameters: See Table 2

1500	      -  Traditional Algorithm: ECDH

1502	         o  Traditional KEM Algorithm: id-ecDH

1504	         o  ECDH curve: secp384r1

1506	   *  id-MLKEM1024-ECDH-brainpoolP384r1-SHA3-256

1508	      -  OID: 1.3.6.1.5.5.7.6.64

1510	      -  Label: "MLKEM1024-BP384"

1512	      -  ML-KEM variant: ML-KEM-1024

1514	      -  Traditional Algorithm: ECDH

1516	         o  Traditional KEM Algorithm: id-ecDH

1518	         o  ECDH curve: brainpoolP384r1

1520	   *  id-MLKEM1024-X448-SHA3-256

1522	      -  OID: 1.3.6.1.5.5.7.6.65

1524	      -  Label: "MLKEM1024-X448"

1526	      -  ML-KEM variant: ML-KEM-1024

1528	      -  Traditional Algorithm: X448

1530	         o  Traditional KEM Algorithm: id-X448

1532	   *  id-MLKEM1024-ECDH-P521-SHA3-256

1534	      -  OID: 1.3.6.1.5.5.7.6.66

1536	      -  Label: "MLKEM1024-P521"

1538	      -  ML-KEM variant: ML-KEM-1024

1540	      -  Traditional Algorithm: ECDH

1542	         o  Traditional KEM Algorithm: id-ecDH
1543	         o  ECDH curve: secp521r1

1545	   In alignment with ML-KEM [FIPS.203], Composite KEM algorithms output
1546	   a 256-bit shared secret key at all security levels.

1548	   For all RSA key types and sizes, the exponent is RECOMMENDED to be
1549	   65537.  Implementations MAY support only 65537 and reject other
1550	   exponent values.  Legacy RSA implementations that use other values
1551	   for the exponent MAY be used within a composite, but need to be
1552	   careful when interoperating with other implementations.

1554	   SHA3-256 is used as the KDF for all Composite ML-KEM algorithms.

1556	6.1.  RSA-OAEP Parameters

1558	   Use of RSA-OAEP [RFC8017] requires additional parameters to be
1559	   specified.

1561	   The RSA component keys MUST be generated at the specified 2048-bit,
1562	   3072-bit, 4096-bit key sizes respectively (up to small differences
1563	   such as dropping leading zeros); intermediate sizes are not
1564	   acceptable.

1566	   As with the other Composite ML-KEM algorithms, AlgorithmIdentifier
1567	   parameters MUST be absent.  The RSA-OAEP primitive SHALL be
1568	   instantiated with the following hard-coded parameters which are the
1569	   same for the 2048, 3072 and 4096 bit key sizes since the objective is
1570	   to carry and output a 256-bit shared secret key at all security
1571	   levels.

1573	             +=============================+=================+
1574	             | RSAES-OAEP-params           | Value           |
1575	             +=============================+=================+
1576	             | hashAlgorithm               | id-sha256       |
1577	             +-----------------------------+-----------------+
1578	             | MaskGenAlgorithm.algorithm  | id-mgf1         |
1579	             +-----------------------------+-----------------+
1580	             | maskGenAlgorithm.parameters | id-sha256       |
1581	             +-----------------------------+-----------------+
1582	             | pSourceAlgorithm            | pSpecifiedEmpty |
1583	             +-----------------------------+-----------------+
1584	             | ss_len                      | 256 bits        |
1585	             +-----------------------------+-----------------+

1587	                        Table 2: RSA-OAEP Parameters

1589	   Full specifications for the referenced algorithms can be found in
1590	   Appendix B.

1592	   Note: The mask length, according to [RFC8017], is k - hLen - 1, where
1593	   k is the size of the RSA modulus.  Since the choice of hash function
1594	   and the RSA key size is fixed for each composite algorithm,
1595	   implementations could choose to pre-compute and hard-code the mask
1596	   length.

1598	6.2.  Rationale for choices

1600	   In generating the list of composite algorithms, the idea was to
1601	   provide composite algorithms at various security levels with varying
1602	   performance characteristics.

1604	   The main design consideration in choosing pairings is to prioritize
1605	   providing pairings of each ML-KEM security level with commonly-
1606	   deployed traditional algorithms.  This supports the design goal of
1607	   using composites as a stepping stone to efficiently deploy post-
1608	   quantum on top of existing hardened and certified traditional
1609	   algorithm implementations.  This was prioritized rather than
1610	   attempting to exactly match the security level of the post-quantum
1611	   and traditional components -- which in general is difficult to do
1612	   since there is no academic consensus on how to compare the "bits of
1613	   security" against classical attackers and "qubits of security"
1614	   against quantum attackers.

1616	   While it may seem odd to use 256-bit outputs at all security levels,
1617	   this aligns with ML-KEM [FIPS.203] which produces a 256-bit shared
1618	   secret key at all security levels.  SHA3-256 has 256 bits of (2nd)
1619	   pre-image resistance, which is the required property for a KDF to
1620	   provide 128 bits of security, as allowed in Table 3 of
1621	   [SP.800-57pt1r5].

1623	7.  ASN.1 Module

1625	   <CODE STARTS>
