1977	9.  Security Considerations

1979	   The primary security considerations when implementing a composite
1980	   algorithm are to ensure that the security considerations of all
1981	   component algorithms have been adhered to, including all
1982	   recommendations for private key storage and error handling.

1984	9.1.  Why Hybrids?

1986	   In broad terms, a PQ/T Hybrid can be used either to provide dual-
1987	   algorithm security or to provide migration flexibility.  Let's
1988	   quickly explore both.

1990	   Dual-algorithm security.  The general idea is that the data is
1991	   protected by two algorithms such that an attacker would need to break
1992	   both in order to compromise the data.  As with most of cryptography,
1993	   this property is easy to state in general terms, but becomes more
1994	   complicated when expressed in formalisms.  The following sections go
1995	   into more detail here.

1997	   Migration flexibility.  Some PQ/T hybrids exist to provide a sort of
1998	   "OR" mode where the application can choose to use one algorithm or
1999	   the other or both.  The intention is that the PQ/T hybrid mechanism
2000	   builds in application backwards compatibility to allow legacy and
2001	   upgraded applications to co-exist and communicate.  The composite
2002	   algorithms presented in this specification do not provide this since
2003	   they operate in a strict "AND" mode.  They do, however, provide
2004	   codebase migration flexibility.  Consider that an organization has
2005	   today a mature, validated, certified, hardened implementation of RSA
2006	   or ECC; composites allow them to add an ML-KEM implementation which
2007	   immediately starts providing benefits against harvest-now-decrypt-
2008	   later attacks even if that ML-KEM implementation is still an
2009	   experimental, non-validated, non-certified, non-hardened
2010	   implementation.  More considerations around FIPS certification of a
2011	   composite algorithm can be found in Appendix D.

2013	9.2.  KEM Combiner

2015	   The KEM combiner from Section 3.4 is reproduced here for reference.

2017	     SHA3-256(mlkemSS || tradSS || tradCT || tradPK || Label)

2019	                    Figure 3: KEM combiner construction

2021	   The primary security property of the KEM combiner is that it
2022	   preserves indistinguishable (adaptive) chosen-ciphertext (IND-CCA2)
2023	   security of the overall Composite ML-KEM so long as at least one
2024	   component is IND-CCA2 [X-Wing] [GHP18].  Additionally, we also need
2025	   to consider the case where one of the component algorithms is
2026	   completely broken; that the private key is known to an attacker.  In
2027	   this case, we rely on the construction of the KEM combiner to ensure
2028	   that the value of the other shared secret key cannot be leaked or the
2029	   combined shared secret key predicted via manipulation of the broken
2030	   algorithm.

2032	   Each registered Composite ML-KEM algorithm specifies a Label -- see
2033	   Section 6.  Given that each Composite ML-KEM algorithm fully
2034	   specifies the component algorithms, including for example the size of
2035	   the RSA modulus, all inputs to the KEM combiner are fixed-size and
2036	   thus do not require length-prefixing.

2038	   *  mlkemSS is always 32 bytes.

2040	   *  tradSS in the case of DH this is derived by the decapsulator and
2041	      therefore the length is not controlled by the attacker, however in
2042	      the case of RSA-OAEP this value is directly chosen by the sender
2043	      and both the length and content could be freely chosen by an
2044	      attacker.

2046	   *  tradCT is either an elliptic curve public key or an RSA-OAEP
2047	      ciphertext which is required to have its length checked by step 1b
2048	      of RSAES-OAEP-DECRYPT in [RFC8017].

2050	   *  tradPK is the public key of the traditional component (elliptic
2051	      curve or RSA) and therefore fixed-length.

2053	   *  Label is a fixed value specified in this document.

2055	9.2.1.  Mini glossary of KEM security notions

2057	   This mini-glossary contains definitions of security notions that are
2058	   relevant for the security considerations sections that follow.

2060	   *IND-CCA2*: indistinguishability under adaptive chosen ciphertext
2061	   attack [IND-CCA]. *C2PRI*: second ciphertext preimage resistance.  A
2062	   property defined in [X-Wing] that it is computationally difficult to
2063	   find two different ciphertexts that produce the same shared secret
2064	   key. *QSF framework*: the hybrid KEM construction defined in [X-Wing]
2065	   that combines an IND-CCA2 KEM with a C2PRI DH scheme via the combiner
2066	   SHA3-256(mlkemSS || tradSS || tradCT || tradPK || Label).  The QSF
2067	   framework is used as the basis for this specification with only
2068	   cosmetic differences such as moving the label to the end in order to
2069	   be FIPS-compliant (whereas X-Wing has it at the beginning). *Binding
2070	   properties*: a hierarchy of properties introduced in [CDM24] of the
2071	   form X-BIND-P-Q were 𝑋 ∈ {HON, LEAK, MAL} indicates the strength of
2072	   the attacker, and P, Q ∈ {PK, CT, K} indicates that for a given P, it
2073	   is computationally difficult to find a collision in Q with respect to
2074	   decapsulation with the KEM.

2076	9.2.2.  IND-CCA2 Security of the hybrid scheme

2078	   Informally, a Composite ML-KEM algorithm is secure if the combiner
2079	   (SHA3) is secure, and either ML-KEM is secure or the traditional
2080	   component (RSA-OAEP, ECDH, X25519 or X448) is secure.

2082	   The security of ML-KEM and DH hybrids is covered in [X-Wing] and
2083	   requires that the first KEM component (ML-KEM in this construction)
2084	   is IND-CCA2 and second ciphertext preimage resistant (C2PRI) and that
2085	   the DH component is nominal group; i.e. a well-behaved elliptic curve
2086	   DH group, but does not require the traditional component to be IND-
2087	   CCA.  This design choice improves performance by not including the
2088	   large ML-KEM public key and ciphertext, but means that an
2089	   implementation error in the ML-KEM component that affects the
2090	   ciphertext check step of the Fujisaki-Okamoto fujisaki (FO) transform
2091	   could result in the overall composite no longer achieving IND-CCA2
2092	   security.  This solution remains IND-CCA2 due to binding the tradPK
2093	   and tradCT in the KEM combiner.

2095	   The QSF framework presented in [X-Wing] is extended to cover RSA-OAEP
2096	   as the traditional algorithm in place of DH.  Informally we note that
2097	   that RSA-OAEP is IND-CCA2 secure [RFC8017] but is not C2PRI(aka
2098	   ciphertext binding) or public key binding since it is mathematically
2099	   possibly to construct two RSA-OAEP ciphertexts that decapsulate to
2100	   the same shared secret under the same public key or under different
2101	   public keys.  Binding the RSA-OAEP ciphertext and public key to the
2102	   internal KDF restores these properties.  Formally, [Starhunters]
2103	   ports the proof of [X-Wing] to cover RSA-OAEP as the traditional
2104	   component in a QSF construction.  [KWW2026] goes further, analyzing a
2105	   range of different RSA-based KEMs, including the RSA-OAEP-KEM
2106	   construction used in this specification, concluding that it achieves
2107	   LEAK-BIND-K,PK-CT and C2PRI when the ciphertext is included in the
2108	   post-processing KDF.

2110	   The combiner from the QSF framework cannot be assumed to be secure
2111	   when used with other KEMs not covered by the above analysis.  For
2112	   example, see [I-D.irtf-cfrg-hybrid-kems] for a survey of different
2113	   KEM combiners offering different security properties.

2115	9.2.3.  Second pre-image resistance of component KEMs

2117	   The notion of a "ciphertext second pre-image resistant KEM" is
2118	   defined in [X-Wing] as being the property that it is computationally
2119	   difficult to find two different ciphertexts c != c' that will
2120	   decapsulate to the same shared secret key under the same public key.
2121	   For the purposes of a hybrid KEM combiner, this property means that
2122	   given two composite ciphertexts (c1, c2) and (c1', c2'), we must
2123	   obtain a unique overall shared secret key so long as either c1 != c1'
2124	   or c2 != c2' -- i.e. the overall Composite ML-KEM is ciphertext
2125	   second pre-image resistant, and therefore secure so long as one of
2126	   the component KEMs is secure.

2128	   In [X-Wing] it is proven that ML-KEM is a second pre-image resistant
2129	   KEM and therefore the ML-KEM ciphertext can safely be omitted from
2130	   the KEM combiner.  Note that this makes a fundamental assumption on
2131	   ML-KEM remaining ciphertext second pre-image resistant, and therefore
2132	   this formulation of KEM combiner does not fully protect against
2133	   implementation errors in the ML-KEM component -- particularly around
2134	   the ciphertext check step of the Fujisaki-Okamoto transform -- which
2135	   could trivially lead to second ciphertext pre-image attacks that
2136	   break the IND-CCA2 security of the ML-KEM component and of the
2137	   overall Composite ML-KEM.  This could be more fully mitigated by
2138	   binding the ML-KEM ciphertext in the combiner, but a design decision
2139	   was made to settle for protection against algorithmic attacks and not
2140	   implementation attacks against ML-KEM in order to increase
2141	   performance.

2143	   However, since neither RSA-OAEP nor DH guarantee second pre-image
2144	   resistance at all, even in a correct implementation, these
2145	   ciphertexts are bound to the key derivation in order to guarantee
2146	   that c != c' will yield a unique ciphertext, and thus restoring
2147	   second pre-image resistance to the overall Composite ML-KEM.

2149	9.2.4.  Applicability to other component algorithms

2151	   It should be clear that the security analysis of the presented KEM
2152	   combiner construction relies heavily on the specific choices of
2153	   component algorithms and combiner KDF, and this combiner construction
2154	   SHOULD NOT be applied to any other combination of ciphers without
2155	   performing the appropriate security analysis.

2157	9.3.  Key Reuse

2159	   While conformance with this specification requires that both
2160	   components of a composite key MUST be freshly generated, the
2161	   designers are aware that some implementers may be forced to break
2162	   this rule due to operational constraints.  This section documents the
2163	   implications of doing so.

2165	   When using single-algorithm cryptography, the best practice is to
2166	   always generate fresh keying material for each purpose, for obtaining
2167	   both a TLS and S/MIME certificate for the same device.  However, in
2168	   practice key reuse in such scenarios is not always catastrophic to
2169	   security and therefore often tolerated.  However this reasoning does
2170	   not hold in the PQ/T hybrid setting.

2172	   Within the broader context of PQ/T hybrids, we need to consider new
2173	   attack surfaces that arise due to the hybrid constructions and did
2174	   not exist in single-algorithm contexts.  One of these is key reuse
2175	   where the component keys within a hybrid are also used by themselves
2176	   within a single-algorithm context.  For example, it might be tempting
2177	   for an operator to take already-deployed RSA keys and add an ML-KEM
2178	   key to them to form a hybrid.  Within a hybrid signature context this
2179	   leads to a class of attacks referred to as "stripping attacks" where
2180	   one component signature can be extracted and presented as a single-
2181	   algorithm signature.  Hybrid KEMs using a concatenation-style KEM
2182	   combiner, as is done in this specification, do not have the analogous
2183	   attack surface because even if an attacker is able to extract and
2184	   decrypt one of the component ciphertexts, this will yield a different
2185	   shared secret key than the overall shared secret key derived from the
2186	   composite, so any subsequent symmetric cryptographic operations will
2187	   fail.

2189	9.3.1.  Guidance for CAs checking for previous key revocation

2191	   There is a further implication to key reuse regarding certificate
2192	   revocation.  Upon receiving a new certificate enrolment request, many
2193	   certification authorities will check if the requested public key has
2194	   been previously revoked due to key compromise.  Often a CA will
2195	   perform this check by using the public key hash.  Therefore, if one,
2196	   or even both, components of a composite have been previously revoked,
2197	   the CA might only check the hash of the combined composite key and
2198	   not find the revocations.  Therefore, because the possibility of key
2199	   reuse exists even though forbidden in this specification, CAs
2200	   performing revocation checks on a composite key SHOULD also check
2201	   both component keys independently to verify that the component keys
2202	   have not been revoked.

2204	9.4.  Policy for Deprecated and Acceptable Algorithms

2206	   Traditionally, a public key or certificate contains a single
2207	   cryptographic algorithm.  If and when an algorithm becomes deprecated
2208	   (for example, RSA-512, or SHA1), the path to deprecating it through
2209	   policy and removing it from operational environments is, at least in
2210	   principle, straightforward.

2212	   In the composite model this is less obvious since a PQ/T hybrid is
2213	   expected to still be considered valid after the traditional component
2214	   is deprecated for individual use.  As such, a single composite public
2215	   key or certificate may contain a mixture of deprecated and non-
2216	   deprecated algorithms.  In general this should be manageable through
2217	   policy by removing OIDs for the standalone component algorithms while
2218	   still allowing OIDs for composite algorithms.  However, complications
2219	   may arise when the composite implementation needs to invoke the
2220	   cryptographic module for a deprecated component algorithm.  In
2221	   particular, this could lead to complex Cryptographic Bills of
2222	   Materials that show implementations of deprecated algorithms still
2223	   present and being used.

2225	10.  Operational Considerations

2227	10.1.  Combiner Function

2229	   For reference, the KEM combiner used in Composite ML-KEM is:

2231	   ss = SHA3-256(mlkemSS || tradSS || tradCT || tradPK || Label)

2233	   NIST SP 800-227 [SP800-227] allows hybrid key combiners of the
2234	   following form:

2236	   K <- KDM((S1,S2,...,St), OtherInput)           (14)
2237	   Composite ML-KEM maps cleanly into this since it places the two
2238	   shared secret keys mlkemSS || tradSS at the beginning of the KDF
2239	   input such that all other inputs tradCT || tradPK || Label can be
2240	   considered part of OtherInput for the purposes of FIPS certification.

2242	   For the detailed steps of the Key Derivation Mechanism KDM,
2243	   [SP800-227] refers to [SP.800-56Cr2].

2245	   Compliance of the Composite ML-KEM variants is achieved in the
2246	   following way:

2248	   The Composite ML-KEM algorithms use SHA3, and so can be certified
2249	   under [SP.800-56Cr2] One-Step Key Derivation Option 1: H(x) =
2250	   hash(x).

2252	   [SP.800-56Cr2] section 4 "One-Step Key Derivation" requires a counter
2253	   which begins at the 4-byte value 0x00000001.  However, the counter is
2254	   allowed to be omitted when the hash function is executed only once,
2255	   as specified on page 159 of the FIPS 140-3 Implementation Guidance
2256	   [FIPS-140-3-IG].

2258	10.1.1.  Order of KDF inputs with Non-Approved Algorithms

2260	   [SP800-227] adds an important stipulation that was not present in
2261	   earlier NIST specifications:

2263	      This publication approves the use of the key combiner (14) for any
2264	      t > 1, so long as at least one shared secret (i.e., S_j for some
2265	      j) is a shared secret generated from the key- establishment
2266	      methods of SP 800-56A or SP 800-56B, or an approved KEM.

2268	   This means that although Composite ML-KEM always places the shared
2269	   secret key from ML-KEM in the first slot, a Composite ML-KEM can be
2270	   FIPS certified so long as either component is FIPS certified.  This
2271	   is important for several reasons.  First, in the early stages of PQC
2272	   migration, composites allow for a non-FIPS certified ML-KEM
2273	   implementation to be added to a module that already has a FIPS
2274	   certified traditional component, and the resulting composite can be
2275	   FIPS certified.  Second, when eventually RSA and Elliptic Curve are
2276	   no longer FIPS-allowed, the composite can retain its FIPS certified
2277	   status on the strength of the ML-KEM component.  Third, while this is
2278	   outside the scope of this specification, the general composite
2279	   construction could be used to create FIPS certified algorithms that
2280	   contain a component algorithm from a different jurisdiction.  Fourth,
2281	   a composite where both components are FIPS-certified could allow an
2282	   implementer to patch one component algorithm while awaiting re-
2283	   certification while continuing to use the overall composite in FIPS
2284	   mode.

2286	   Note that before [SP800-227] was in force, [SP.800-56Cr2] required
2287	   the shared secret key from the certified algorithm to be in the first
2288	   slot and therefore a Composite ML-KEM implementation using a FIPS-
2289	   certified traditional component and a non-FIPS certified ML-KEM is
2290	   not believed to be certifiable under [SP.800-56Cr2] alone, and
2291	   requires the ammendments made by [SP800-227].

2293	10.2.  Backwards Compatibility

2295	   The term "application backwards compatibility" is used here to mean
2296	   that existing systems as they are deployed today can interoperate
2297	   with the upgraded systems of the future.  This draft explicitly does
2298	   not provide application backwards compatibility, only upgraded
2299	   systems will understand the OIDs defined in this specification.

2301	   These migration and interoperability concerns need to be thought
2302	   about in the context of various types of protocols that make use of
2303	   X.509 and PKIX with relation to key establishment and content
2304	   encryption, from online negotiated protocols such as TLS 1.3
2305	   [RFC8446] and IKEv2 [RFC7296], to non-negotiated asynchronous
2306	   protocols such as S/MIME signed email [RFC8551], as well as myriad
2307	   other standardized and proprietary protocols and applications that
2308	   leverage CMS [RFC5652] encrypted structures.

2310	10.3.  Profiling down the number of options

2312	   One daunting aspect of this specification is the number of composite
2313	   algorithm combinations.  Each option has been specified because there
2314	   is a community that has a direct application for it; typically
2315	   because the traditional component is already deployed in a change-
2316	   managed environment, or because that specific traditional component
2317	   is required for regulatory reasons.

2319	   However, this large number of combinations leads either to fracturing
2320	   of the ecosystem into non-interoperable sub-groups when different
2321	   communities choose non-overlapping subsets to support, or on the
2322	   other hand it leads to spreading development resources too thin when
2323	   trying to support all options.

2325	   This specification does not list any particular composite algorithm
2326	   as mandatory-to-implement, however organizations that operate within
2327	   specific application domains are encouraged to define profiles that
2328	   select a small number of composites appropriate for that application
2329	   domain.  For applications that do not have any regulatory
2330	   requirements or legacy implementations to consider, it is RECOMMENDED
2331	   to focus implementation effort on:

2333	   id-MLKEM768-X25519-SHA3-256  (aka "X-Wing")
2334	   id-MLKEM768-ECDH-P256-SHA3-256

2336	   In applications that only allow NIST PQC Level 5, it is RECOMMENDED
2337	   to focus implementation effort on:

2339	   id-MLKEM1024-ECDH-P384-SHA3-256

2341	10.4.  Decapsulation Requires the Public Key

2343	   ML-KEM always requires the public key in order to perform various
2344	   steps of the Fujisaki-Okamoto decapsulation [FIPS.203], and for this
2345	   reason the private key encoding specified in FIPS 203 includes the
2346	   public key.

2348	   Moreover, the KEM combiner as specified in Section 3.4 requires the
2349	   public key of the traditional component in order to achieve the
2350	   public-key binding property and ciphertext collision resistance as
2351	   described in Section 9.2.  Since tradPK is not carried in the
2352	   composite private key encoding, the implementation is required to
2353	   obtain it from some out-of-band mechanism.  This section discusses
2354	   several options, but is a non-normative, non-exhaustive list.

2356	   1.  Derive or extract from private key.  Many cryptographic modules
2357	       expose functionality to obtain an RSA or EC public key from the
2358	       corresponding private key.  For applications where such
2359	       functionality does not exist, Section 10.4.1 and Section 10.4.2
2360	       provide the suggested mechanisms for extracting the public keys
2361	       from private keys for RSA and ECDH respectively.  It is assumed
2362	       that this is not required for X25519 or X448 since those private
2363	       keys are seeds from which the public key can be obtained.

2365	   2.  Fetch it from an external data source, for example from the
2366	       public-key certificate corresponding to this private key.

2368	   3.  If the composite KEM private key is being carried within a PKCS#8
2369	       OneAsymmetricKey object, place the full composite public key
2370	       within the optional OneAsymmetricKey.publicKey field, which
2371	       allows extracting the tradPK (and re-encode as necessary for
2372	       correctly using it in the KEM Combiner).

2374	   4.  Use an alternate private key encoding that explicitly carries the
2375	       tradPK.

2377	10.4.1.  Extracting RSAPublicKey from RSAPrivateKey

2379	   Assuming that the RSA component of the composite private key is
2380	   encoded as an RSAPrivateKey, as required by this specification, then,
2381	   quoting from [RFC8017] you have:

2383	   RSAPrivateKey ::= SEQUENCE {
2384	       version           Version,
2385	       modulus           INTEGER,  -- n
2386	       publicExponent    INTEGER,  -- e
2387	       privateExponent   INTEGER,  -- d
2388	       prime1            INTEGER,  -- p
2389	       prime2            INTEGER,  -- q
2390	       exponent1         INTEGER,  -- d mod (p-1)
2391	       exponent2         INTEGER,  -- d mod (q-1)
2392	       coefficient       INTEGER,  -- (inverse of q) mod p
2393	       otherPrimeInfos   OtherPrimeInfos OPTIONAL
2394	   }

2396	   This can trivially be converted into an RSAPublicKey through simple
2397	   DER decoding / re-encoding since both required values are already
2398	   present.

2400	   RSAPublicKey ::= SEQUENCE {
2401	       modulus           INTEGER,  -- n
2402	       publicExponent    INTEGER   -- e
2403	   }

2405	10.4.2.  Deriving the public ECPoint from ECPrivateKey

2407	   Unlike RSA, the ECPrivateKey does not contain sufficient information
2408	   to simply extract the public key.  Note that in the interest of
2409	   having a single unique encoding to foster interoperability, this
2410	   specification forbids the optional publicKey field.

2412	   That said, the EC public key can be derived from the private key in
2413	   the following way:

2415	   g = generator for the group P256r1, P384r1, etc.
2416	   s = ECPrivateKey.getS()

2418	   pubKey = ec_multiply_by_scalar(g, s)

2420	   where a recommended implementation of ec_multiply_by_scalar() can be
2421	   found in [SEC1].

2423	   Then encode pubKey as X9.62 uncompressed point.

2425	10.5.  Interoperability of legacy algorithms

2427	   The legacy component algorithms, particularly RSA and ECDSA can
2428	   themselves have interoperability issues which will propagate to
2429	   become interoperability issues in the composite.  For example, this
2430	   specification RECOMMENDS an RSA exponent of 65537, but other values
2431	   are possible.  Similarly, due to the details of DER encoding, keys
2432	   that happen to have leading zeros could appear to be smaller than the
2433	   required key size even though they are actually acceptable.

2435	   Implementations are encouraged to be lenient when parsing the key
2436	   material of the legacy algorithm.  In particular, the recommendation
2437	   is to use existing implementations of the legacy algorithms that
2438	   already handle all the variations seen in the wild.

2729	Appendix A.  Maximum Key and Ciphertext Sizes

2731	   The sizes listed below are maximum values: several factors could
2732	   cause fluctuations in the size of the traditional component.  For
2733	   example, this could be due to:

2735	   *  The RSA public key (n, e) allows e to vary in size between 3 and n
2736	      - 1 [RFC8017].  Note that the size table below assumes the
2737	      recommended value of e = 65537, so for RSA combinations it is in
2738	      fact not a true maximum.

2740	   *  When the underlying RSA or EC value is itself DER-encoded, integer
2741	      values could occasionally be shorter than expected due to leading
2742	      zeros being dropped from the encoding.

2744	   By contrast, ML-KEM values are always fixed size, so composite values
2745	   can always be correctly de-serialized based on the size of the ML-KEM
2746	   component.

2748	   Size values marked with an asterisk (*) in the table are not fixed
2749	   but maximum possible values for the composite key or ciphertext.
2750	   Implementations MUST NOT perform strict length checking based on such
2751	   values.

2753	   Non-hybrid ML-KEM is included for reference.

2755	    +============================+========+=========+============+====+
2756	    | Algorithm                  | Public | Private | Ciphertext | SS |
2757	    |                            | key    | key     |            |    |
2758	    +============================+========+=========+============+====+

2852	Appendix C.  Fixed Component Algorithm Identifiers

2854	   Many cryptographic libraries are X.509-focused and do not expose
2855	   interfaces to instantiate a public key from raw bytes, but only from
2856	   a SubjectPublicKeyInfo structure as you would find in an X.509
2857	   certificate, therefore implementing Composite ML-KEM in those
2858	   libraries requires reconstructing the SPKI for each component
2859	   algorithm.  In order to aid implementers and reduce interoperability
2860	   issues, this section lists out the full public key for each component
2861	   algorithm.

2863	   *ML-KEM-768*

2865	   ASN.1:
2866	     algorithm AlgorithmIdentifier ::= {
2867	       algorithm id-alg-ml-kem-768   -- (2.16.840.1.101.3.4.4.2)
2868	       }

2870	   DER:
2871	     30 0B 06 07 60 86 48 01 65 03 04 04 02

2873	   *ML-KEM-1024*

2875	   ASN.1:

2877	   ASN.1:
2878	     algorithm AlgorithmIdentifier ::= {
2879	       algorithm id-alg-ml-kem-1024   -- (2.16.840.1.101.3.4.4.3)
2880	       }

2882	   DER:
2883	     30 0B 06 07 60 86 48 01 65 03 04 04 03

2885	   *RSA-OAEP - all sizes*
2886	   ASN.1:
2887	     algorithm AlgorithmIdentifier ::= {
2888	       algorithm id-RSAES-OAEP,   -- (1.2.840.113549.1.1.7)
2889	       parameters RSAES-OAEP-params {
2890	            hashFunc      [0] id-sha256,  -- (2.16.840.1.101.3.4.2.1)
2891	            maskGenFunc   [1] mgf1SHA256Identifier,

2893	       }

2895	   where

2900	         sha256Identifier  AlgorithmIdentifier  ::=  { id-sha256, NULL }

3001	Appendix D.  FIPS Certification

3003	   The following sections give guidance to implementers wishing to FIPS-
3004	   certify a composite implementation.

3006	   This guidance is not authoritative and has not been endorsed by NIST.

3008	   Implementers seeking FIPS certification of a composite KEM algorithm
3009	   where only one of the component algorithms has been FIPS-validated or
3010	   FIPS-approved should credit the FIPS-validated component algorithm
3011	   with full security strength, the non-FIPS-validated component
3012	   algorithm with zero security, and the overall composite should be
3013	   considered at least as strong and thus FIPS-approved.

3015	   The composite algorithm has been designed to treat the underlying
3016	   primitives as "black-box implementations" and not impose any
3017	   additional requirements on them that could require an existing
3018	   implementation of an underlying primitive to run in a mode different
3019	   from the one under which it was certified.  For example, the KeyGen
3020	   defined in Section 3.1 invokes ML-KEM.KeyGen_internal(d, z) which
3021	   might not be available in a cryptographic module running in FIPS-
3022	   mode, but Section 3.1 is only a suggested implementation and the
3023	   composite KeyGen MAY be implemented using a different available
3024	   interface for ML-KEM.KeyGen.

3026	   The authors wish to note that composite algorithms provide a design
3027	   pattern to provide utility in future situations that require care to
3028	   remain FIPS-compliant, such as future cryptographic migrations as
3029	   well as bridging across jurisdictions with non-intersecting
3030	   cryptographic requirements.

3032	   Successful FIPS certification will need to take into account the
3033	   "Encapsulation key check" in section 7.2 of [FIPS.203] and the
3034	   "Decapsulation input check" in section 7.3 of [FIPS.203] which are
3035	   required for correct and secure functioning of ML-KEM, but which are
3036	   considered to be external to the Encaps() and Decaps() algorithms.

3038	   The following sections go into further detail on specific issues that
3039	   relate to FIPS certification.

3041	Appendix E.  Comparison with other Hybrid KEMs

3043	E.1.  X-Wing

3045	   This specification borrows extensively from the analysis and KEM
3046	   combiner construction presented in [X-Wing].  In particular, X-Wing
3047	   and id-MLKEM768-X25519-SHA3-256 (specified in this document) are
3048	   largely interchangeable.  The one difference is that X-Wing uses a
3049	   combined KeyGen function to generate the two component private keys
3050	   from the same seed, which gives some additional binding properties.
3051	   This specification takes a more flexible approach of assuming that
3052	   each component algorithm is generated independently, which is
3053	   expected to be required in many migration scenarios, such as where
3054	   the component primitives are independently validated and certified.
3055	   However, the X-Wing style joint keygen is also allowed, and therefore
3056	   an X-Wing implementation is considered to be a compliant
3057	   implementation of id-MLKEM768-X25519-SHA3-256, but id-
3058	   MLKEM768-X25519-SHA3-256 might not be considered a compliant
3059	   implementation of X-Wing.  See Section 3.1.1 for more discussion.

3061	E.2.  ETSI CatKDF

3063	   [ETSI.TS.103.744] section 8.2.3 defines CatKDF as:

3065	   1) Form secret = psk || k1 || k2.
3066	   2) Set context = f(info, MA, MB), where f is a context formatting
3067	      function.
3068	   3) key_material = KDF(secret, label, context, length).
3069	   4) Return key_material.

3071	   MA shall contain all of the public keys.
3072	   MB shall contain all of the corresponding public keys and ciphertexts

3074	   The main difference between the Composite ML-KEM combiner and the
3075	   ETSI CatKDF combiner is that CatKDF makes the more conservative
3076	   choice to bind the public keys and ciphertexts of both components,
3077	   while Composite ML-KEM follows the analysis presented in [X-Wing]
3078	   that while preserving the security properties of the traditional
3079	   component requires binding the public key and ciphertext of the
3080	   traditional component, it is not necessary to do so for ML-KEM thanks
3081	   to the rejection sampling step of the Fujisaki-Okamoto transform.

3083	   Additionally, ETSI CatKDF can be instantiated with either HMAC
3084	   [RFC2104], KMAC [SP.800-185] or HKDF [RFC5869] as KDF.  Since this
3085	   specification uses SHA3-256 as the KDF for all variants, there is no
3086	   equivalent construction of CatKDF.

3088	Appendix F.  Examples of KEM Combiner Intermediate Values

3090	   This section provides examples of constructing the input for the KEM
3091	   Combiner, showing all intermediate values.  This is intended to be
3092	   useful for debugging purposes.  See Section 3.4 for additional
3093	   information.

3095	   Each input component is shown.  Note that values are shown hex-
3096	   encoded for display purposes only, they are actually raw binary
3097	   values.

3099	   *  mlkemSS is the shared secret produced by the ML-KEM encapsulate or
3100	      decapsulate function which is always 32 bytes.

3102	   *  tradSS is the shared secret produce by the traditional algorithm.

3104	   *  tradCT is either an elliptic curve public key or an RSA-OAEP
3105	      ciphertext depending on the algorithm chosen.

3107	   *  tradPK is the public key of the traditional component (elliptic
3108	      curve or RSA) and therefore fixed-length.

3110	   *  Label is the specific KEM Combiner Label for this composite
3111	      algorithm.  See Section 6

3113	   Next, the Combined KDF Input is given, which is simply the
3114	   concatenation of the above values.

3116	   Finally, the KDF Function and the ss Output are shown as outputs.
3117	   The ss is the Composite ML-KEM shared-secret generated by applying
3118	   the KDF to the Combined KDF Input.

3120	   Examples are given for each recommended Composite ML-KEM algorithm
3121	   from Section 10.3.

3123	   Example 1:

3125	   Example of id-MLKEM768-ECDH-P256-SHA3-256 Combiner function output.

3238	   # ss = SHA3-256(Combined KDF Input)

3240	   ss:
3241	   eb60f6c80a309ad4158d7b02f2cf8c947faead96ebbd85c3f62a94868ffddca4

3243	Appendix G.  Test Vectors

3245	   The following test vectors are provided in a format similar to the
3246	   NIST ACVP Known-Answer-Tests (KATs).

3248	   The structure is that a global cacert is provided which is used to
3249	   sign each KEM certificate.

3251	   Within each test case there are the following values:

3253	   *  tcId the name of the algorithm.

3255	   *  ek the encapsulation public key.

3257	   *  x5c the X.509 certificate of the encapsulation key, signed by the
3258	      cacert.

3260	   *  dk the raw decapsulation private key.

3262	   *  dk_pkcs8 the decapsulation private key in a PKCS#8 object.

3264	   *  c the ciphertext.

3266	   *  k the derived shared secret key.

3268	   Implementers should be able to perform the following tests using the
3269	   test vectors below:

3271	   1.  Load the public key ek or certificate x5c and perform an
3272	       encapsulation for it (you should obtain valid ct and k values,
3273	       but they will not match the ones in the test vector since
3274	       Encaps() is randomized.)

3276	   2.  Load the decapsulation private key dk or dk_pkcs8 and the
3277	       ciphertext c and perform a Decaps() operation to ensure that the
3278	       same shared secret key k is derived.

3280	   Test vectors are provided for each underlying ML-KEM algorithm in
3281	   isolation for the purposes of debugging.

3283	   Due to the length of the test vectors, some readers will prefer to
3284	   retrieve the non-word-wrapped copy from GitHub [TestVectors].  The
3285	   reference implementation written in python that generated them is
3286	   also available.

6048	Appendix H.  Contributors and Acknowledgments

6050	   This document represents the results of a many-year effort by the
6051	   LAMPS working group.  Over that time the following working group
6052	   members provided valuable review and commentary on the document:

6054	   Serge Mister (Entrust), Felipe Ventura (Entrust), Richard Kettlewell
6055	   (Entrust), Ali Noman (Entrust), Peter C.  (UK NCSC), Tim Hollebeek
6056	   (Digicert), Sophie Schmieg (Google), Deirdre Connolly (SandboxAQ),
6057	   Chris A.  Wood (Apple), Bas Westerbaan (Cloudflare), Falko Strenzke
6058	   (MTG AG), Piotr Popis (Enigma), Jean-Pierre Fiset (Crypto4A), Carl
6059	   Wallace, Daniel Van Geest (CryptoNext Security), 陳志華 (Abel C.  H.
6060	   Chen, Chunghwa Telecom), 林邦曄 (Austin Lin, Chunghwa Telecom) and
6061	   Douglas Stebila (University of Waterloo).

6063	   We wish to acknowledge a few people who have made notable
6064	   contributions to specific sections of this document.

6066	   We wish to acknowledge particular effort from Carl Wallace and Daniel
6067	   Van Geest (CryptoNext Security), who have implemented each successive
6068	   version of the draft over multiple years to provide valuable
6069	   implementation experience and hackathon testing.  Thanks to Stepan
6070	   Yakimovich for contributing to the reference implementation to be
6071	   able to provide and verify hackathon artifacts.

6073	   Thanks to Giacomo Pope (github.com/GiacomoPope) whose ML-DSA and ML-
6074	   KEM implementations were used to generate the test vectors.

6076	   We are grateful to all who have given feedback over the years,
6077	   formally or informally, on mailing lists or in person, including any
6078	   contributors who may have been inadvertently omitted from this list.

6080	   Finally, we wish to thank the authors of all the referenced documents
6081	   upon which this specification was built.  "Copying always makes
6082	   things easier and less error prone" - [RFC8411].
